## Page 1


Persistence of asymptotic variance under transport:
from hyperfluctuation to stealthy hyperuniformity
Luca Lotz1,2 and Michael A. Klatt3,1,4
1German Aerospace Center (DLR), Institute of Frontier Materials on Earth and in Space,
Functional, Granular, and Composite Materials, Linder H¨ohe, 51170 Cologne, Germany
2Institute for Stochastics, Karlsruhe Institute of Technology, Englerstr. 2, 76131 Karlsruhe, Germany.
3German Aerospace Center (DLR), Institute for AI Safety and Security,
Wilhelm-Runge-Str. 10, 89081 Ulm, Germany
4Department of Physics, Ludwig-Maximilians-Universit¨at M¨unchen,
Schellingstr. 4, 80799 Munich, Germany
May 2026
Abstract
We introduce p-uniformity to characterize the scaling of density fluctuations
in spatial random systems in Rd, ranging from hyperfluctuation to stealthy hy-
peruniformity.
Our central theorem establishes sufficient conditions to preserve
p-uniformity under transport. The first condition, a finite (d + p)-th moment of
the transport distance, allows for a Taylor expansion of the transport. The second
condition controls the corresponding terms. We thus solve a previously stated open
problem; indeed we extend it, since our result applies to a general p-uniform source
in any dimension, and the source and transport may be dependent. As an applica-
tion, we construct new classes of point processes that are isotropic and p-uniform
with arbitrarily high p, and that can be simulated in linear time. We conclude with
an outlook on a converse statement.

Introduction
Let Φ be a stationary random complex measure, e.g., a point process, on Rd that is locally
square-integrable, i.e., E[|Φ(B)|2] < ∞for all bounded Borel sets B ⊆Rd. Let λd denote
the Lebesgue measure on Rd, let f : Rd →[0, ∞) be a test function with ∥f∥2 = 1, and
define fr(x) := f(x

of a ball normalized by its volume, where the ball acts as an ‘observation window’. Alter-
natively, f can be a smooth function with compact support or at least with a sufficiently
fast decay, which represents a ‘diffuse observation window’. The asymptotic variance σ2
Φ
quantifies the mass fluctuations of the random measure in the limit of large observation
windows. For example, if Φ is a point process, σ2
Φ is the limit of the variance of the number
of points in the observation windows rescaled by volume. We are especially interested in
the case σ2
Φ = 0, where mass fluctuations are suppressed at large scales. Such a random
measure is called hyperuniform [92, 89].
Prominent examples of hyperuniform point processes include lattices and quasicrys-
tals [92, 73, 8], as well as ergodic, isotropic, and locally disordered examples [89], e.g.,
2


## Page 3


determinantal point processes [85] or the one-component plasma [66]. Hyperuniformity
is of great interest to physics [30, 92, 27, 90, 89, 37, 77, 98, 31, 83, 67, 95], materials
science [35, 104, 102, 56]. It also increasingly attracts interest in mathematics [32, 33, 58,
68, 21, 8, 62, 14, 61, 26, 43].
Recently, a particular focus has lain on the construction and simulation of large-scale
samples of isotropic amorphous (i.e., non-crystalline) hyperuniform point processes (for
d ≥2) [93, 94, 3, 83, 87]. Such algorithms are especially important for the study of
hyperuniformity because it is a long-range property and hence takes strong advantage of
large system sizes. However, the results were so far mainly limited to numerical studies.
Of special interest are point processes with rapid decay of mass fluctuations in growing
observation windows (which can be observed in (1.1) if f is chosen to be smooth) because
such processes exhibit a strong reduction of fluctuations already at intermediate window
sizes. This behavior extends the range of the corresponding physical effects.
Seemingly independent, the theoretical question about the persistence of hyperunifor-
mity under transport has attracted considerable attention. A random transport K moves
mass from one random measure Φ, the source, to another random measure KΦ, the desti-
nation. Practically, one can think of a random transport as a random movement of mass,
e.g., as a displacement of points. So far, there have been two complementary approaches
to develop conditions that guarantee the persistence of hyperuniformity under transport.
The first establishes the persistence of hyperuniformity under a simple d-th moment con-
dition on the transport distance, but is limited to dimension d ≤2 [21]. The second works
in any dimension, but is limited by a restriction in long-range dependence [26, 55]. How-
ever, both approaches only control the asymptotic variance itself and cannot guarantee
the persistence of the decay rate beyond a very limited degree. A more comprehensive
control of the decay rate is not only of theoretical but also of practical interest, since
it enables the construction of non-crystalline hyperuniform point processes with rapidly
decaying number fluctuations.
Here, we fill this gap. We begin by formalizing an order in the asymptotic behavior of
Var[Φ(fr)]. Usually, this behavior is quantified using the scaling exponent [89, Section 5],
also called the hyperuniformity exponent [68, 60]. However, this scaling exponent is not
well-defined for every stationary random measure Φ. Hence, we introduce the more general
but closely connected concept of p-uniformity in Section 3. Under our assumptions, there
is a unique positive semi-definite signed measure βΦ, called the covariance measure, such
that
Cov[Φ(f), Φ(g)] = βΦ(f ⋆g)
(1.2)
for all bounded functions f, g with bounded support, where ⋆is the tilted convolution
defined as in (2.10). Its Fourier transform ˆβΦ is a locally finite measure and called the
Bartlett spectral measure or diffraction measure [2, 8]. For p ∈[−d, ∞), we call Φ p-
uniform if, as ε →0,
ˆβΦ(Bε)

stealthy hyperuniformity [3, 88]. A similar technique is used in [73], where ˆβΦ(Bε) is
denoted by Z(ε). If Φ is p-uniform and p < ∞, then the fraction on the RHS (right-hand

Another benefit of the notion of p-uniformity is that we can connect it to the results
from [32, 61] to show that (2p + d)-uniformity implies p-rigidity for any p ∈N0 ∪{∞};
see (3.35) or [34, 61] for the definition of rigidity.
Next, we establish conditions under which p-uniformity is preserved under transports;
see Section 4. Hence, we also answer the previously mentioned open question about the
persistence of rapid decay of mass fluctuations. We start with showing that a d-th moment
condition on the transport distance guarantees that local square integrability persists
under transport; see Subsection 4.1 and partially also [21]. Then, in Subsection 4.2, we
show that a (d + p)-th moment condition on the transport distance allows for a form of
Taylor expansion of the transport. Concretely, given the source Φ, the transport K, and
the smooth, compactly supported test function f, the destination KΦ evaluated at the
scaled test function fr (defined as in (1.1)) admits the expansion

4


## Page 5


This connection is no coincidence. Some parts of the proofs even build upon [21].
However, we introduce multiple new crucial elements. The most important is the multiple-
term expansion (1.5), whereas in [21] only the cases are considered where a single term
is sufficient. This allows us to also formulate a condition for the persistence of hyperuni-
formity for d ≥3, which was an open question of theirs. Critically, we also go beyond
hyperuniformity and can ensure the persistence of p-uniformity for higher p. Finally, we
substantially broaden the applicability by allowing the source to be an arbitrary random
complex measure and the transport to be not only spatially correlated with itself but also
dependent on the source.
In Subsection 4.3, we expand on the concept of transport in a more physical way. We
interpolate the source and destination spatially continuously. It turns out that our condi-
tions ensuring the persistence of p-uniformity from the source directly to the destination
also imply that p-uniformity is preserved along the way. Conversely, the persistence of
p-uniformity under spatial interpolation almost implies these conditions.
In Section 5, we primarily apply our results to construct new classes of point processes.
The first, in Subsection 5.1, is the aforementioned class of non-crystalline point processes
for d ≥2 that can be p-uniform for any p. They can even be simulated in linear time,
and the algorithm parallelizes perfectly. Thus, we solve also this previously mentioned
open problem. For the construction, we leverage fair tilings like in [55] to reduce the
global to a local problem. A tiling is called fair if every cell has the same volume. We
construct a new class that can be simulated in linear time. The technique expands on the
STIT-tilings that are created by iteratively dividing cells using random hyperplanes [72,
71]. We deviate by only dividing cells such that both newly created cells have the same
volume. We call the invariant distribution of such a Markov chain on the space of fair
tessellations a fair STIT. If we choose the splitting direction uniformly, the resulting fair
STIT is ergodic and isotropic, i.e., non-crystalline. In the second step, we apply an idea
from [28]. Let n ∈N, p ∈N0. We place (random) points X1, ..., Xn in every cell C such
that the first p moments of the cell and the points coincide, i.e.,

Then we apply our main Theorem 4.13 to show that the resulting point process is beyond
(2p −d)-uniform; see Fig. 1. This procedure can be simulated very efficiently, since each
cell can be treated independently. The locations of the points in each cell can also be
considered to be the nodes of an equal-weight quadrature formula of the cell. These are
specifically called averaging sets or t-designs [81, 19] and connect our construction to
the theory of hyperuniformity in compact spaces [13]. In Appendix C, we contribute to
the theory of averaging sets and, e.g., show that for every d ∈N, p ∈N0, there is an
n0 ∈N such that for every n ≥n0 there is an averaging set of size n and order p for every
bounded convex C ⊆Rd, showing that the previous construction is indeed possible for n
sufficiently large.
By applying our theorems to different fair tilings, we can also show that, under a
moment condition, the arbitrary placement of a singular point in each cell yields a beyond
(2 −d)-uniform point process, and the placement of a singular point in the center of
mass yields a beyond (4 −d)-uniform point process. Hence, the former always yields a

with an exceptionally large scaling exponent. In black and fading out from left to right,
we show a realization of a random fair tiling, i.e., a random division of space into cells of
equal volume; specifically, a realization of a fair STIT; see Subsection 5.1. To obtain the
point process shown in blue, we concentrate the mass that is uniformly distributed over
each cell into n points while preserving the first p moments, i.e., satisfying (1.8). Here,
n = 12 and p = 6; see the cell highlighted in red. By our main Theorem 4.13 about
the persistence of p-uniformity under transport, this point process is beyond (2p −d)-
uniform, i.e., beyond 10-uniform in this case. A simulation study with 50, 000 samples of
around 50, 000 points each yields the estimated structure factor shown in the inset. The
data suggests that the point process is even 11.5-uniform. A point process with such a
large scaling exponent is approximately stealthy within floating-point precision even up
to intermediate wavenumbers ∥k∥. Moreover, the linear scaling of the algorithm enables
the generation of samples several orders of magnitude larger.

## Page 7


hyperuniform point process for d ≤2 (see also [21]), and the latter for d ≤4. As a
special case, we conclude that the centers of mass of the tiles of a pinwheel tiling form a
beyond 2-uniform, hence also class I hyperuniform, point process [76]. For the remaining
dimensions, we construct counterexamples in Appendix D; see partially also [21].
The second class we consider in Subsection 5.2 is similar in nature, but its construc-
tion is based on a progenitor point process instead of a fair tiling. For every point of
the progenitor point process, which is assumed to be ˜p-uniform for some ˜p ≥p, we in-
dependently place a random cluster of new points. Again, these clusters form averaging
sets, but this time not of cells but of some arbitrarily chosen finite measure. In this way,
the ˜p-uniformity of the progenitor point process can be reduced by a controlled amount.
While this construction is a lot simpler, this simplicity allows us to not only prove that the
result is p-uniform for some p ∈2N0, but also to show that this value of p is maximal. As
examples for clusters, we bring up averaging sets of trigonometric manifolds and spherical
t-designs [19]. An example from [61] arises as a special case for d = 2. By generalizing
this principle—placing random finite measures instead of random point clusters—we fur-
ther construct, to the best of our knowledge, the first nonnegative random field that is
∞-uniform, i.e., stealthy hyperuniform, and that also has a continuous structure factor.
In Subsection 5.3, we do not construct a new class of p-uniform point processes. In-
stead, we more generally specialize our main theorem to the case where the transport is
a displacement field. We derive a mixing condition for the persistence of p-uniformity for
p ≤2 under displacement. Our conditions are strictly weaker than those from [26] and
practically also weaker than those from [55]. Then, we prove a more specialized version
for Gaussian displacements independent of the source. There, we also derive a condition
that is strictly weaker than those in [26, 55]. The conditions in our result can now be con-
sidered essentially sharp. We conclude the subsection by rigorously proving for d = 1 that
the destination of a perturbation by a Gaussian random field independent of the source
cannot be beyond 4-uniform under very basic assumptions. This statement was already
formulated in [29], but their argument was not mathematically rigorous and omitted the
necessary conditions. We expect that the assertion also to holds for d ≥2 under suitable
conditions, but the restriction to d = 1 simplifies the proof significantly.
In Subsection 5.4, we apply our results to make another statement from the physics
literature mathematically rigorous for d ≥3. We show that, while a perfect lattice is
∞-uniform, i.e., stealthy hyperuniform, the lattice at positive temperature is not even
beyond 0-uniform, i.e., not hyperuniform. The proof generalizes to all materials with an
asymptotically linear dispersion relation.
In the final Section 6, we give an outlook on how our concepts can be extended in a
different direction. Transports can always be associated with so-called transport costs.
From this perspective, our main theorem implies that a transport that has a low cost
in a certain sense preserves p-uniformity. In particular, when we fix the source to be
the ∞-uniform Lebesgue measure (or lattice like in [21]), it is natural to also ask the
reverse question. Does p-uniformity of a random measure also imply a certain form of
low transport distance to the Lebesgue measure? Here, a low transport distance of two
random measures is synonymous with the existence of a low-cost transport from one to
another. If the statement is true, it will open the possibility to (almost) characterize
p-uniformity in a transport sense. Actually, for d = 2 and p = 0, the question has already
been answered in [62, 43], and in [14] even more generally for d ≥2, d + p ≤2. For
7


## Page 8


the remaining cases, we introduce a new form of transport cost closely connected to the
conditions in our main theorem. We think that this transport cost should be the key to
answering the question conclusively for every dimension d ∈N and every p ∈[−d, ∞).
We support this claim by showing that at least all ∞-uniform, i.e., stealthy hyperuniform,
random measures have this sort of low transport distance to the Lebesgue measure.
2
Preliminaries
In this section, we recall some foundational definitions and results on random measures
and transport that we rely on throughout the paper. More specifically, we are interested
in stationary random measures and their second moment properties, which also involves
the Bartlett spectral measure; see [2]. We allow for the random measures to be signed
(and even complex-valued) since these objects come up naturally in Section 4, even when
we apply the Theorems from there to nonnegative random measures. Further, the physics
community has also been interested in the asymptotic properties of complex-valued ran-
dom measures recently; see, e.g., [91].
Transports are mainly handled in the form of
transport kernels. Most elements can also be found in the Preliminaries of [55], but some
objects are treated in greater generality here. For a more in-depth look into the theory
of random measures, we recommend [49].
2.1
Random complex measures
Let X be a metric space. Most of the time, we simply assume that X = Rd for some
d ∈N. We define M+ := M+(X) as the space of locally finite measures on X. The space
of all locally absolutely finite complex measures on X is then simply defined as
M := M(X) := {µ1 −µ2 + i(µ3 −µ4) : µ1, µ2, µ3, µ4 ∈M+(X)}.
(2.1)
Given µ ∈M(X), its total variation measure is denoted by |µ|. It is the smallest element
of M+(X) such that |µ(B)| ≤|µ|(B) for all B ∈Bb(X), where Bb(X) is the set of all
bounded Borel sets of X. If X = Rd, we also use Bd
b := Bb(Rd). If, e.g., µ ∈M(X) has
a density f : X →C with respect to some ν ∈M+(X), then |µ| has the density |f| with
respect to ν. For a measurable function f : X →C which is integrable with respect to
|µ|, we extend the notation by
µ(f) :=

(2.2)
Proceeding, we equip M(X) with the sigma field M := M(X) generated by the map-
pings M(X) →C; µ 7→µ(B) with B ∈Bb(X). We have M+(X) ∈M(X), and further
prominent examples are the counting measures N := N(X) := {µ ∈N(X) : µ(B) ∈
N0 for B ∈Bb(X)} and simple counting measures Ns := Ns(X) := {µ ∈M(X) : µ({x}) ∈
{0, 1} for x ∈X}; see, e.g., [64, Subsection 2.1].
A random complex measure is a random element Φ of M with respect to some fixed
probability space (Ω, A, P), which is assumed to be rich enough to allow for the construc-
tion of all random elements we consider in this paper. In particular, a random element
of M+ is called (nonnegative) random measure, a random element of N is called point

E[Φ(f)] = E[Φ](f),

Invariance
Going forward, we are mostly interested in the case X = Rd and so-called stationary
random complex measures. A random complex measure Φ on Rd is called stationary if
θxΦ
d= Φ for all x ∈Rd, where θxΦ := Φ −x := Φ(· + x). Sometimes, we also assume
isotropy, which is defined in the same way with the group of shift-operators replaced
by the group of isometries of Rd. To be able to handle both concepts at once, and to
make sure that multiple objects are not only individually but also jointly stationary, we
introduce invariance.
Suppose that G is a group that acts measurably on X and Ω, and preserves P, i.e.,
P(gA) = P(A) for g ∈G, A ∈A. Then a random complex measure Φ on X is called
invariant (with respect to the group action) if
Φ(gω, gB) = Φ(ω, B),
g ∈G, ω ∈Ω, B ∈Bb(X).
(2.4)
If X = Rd, and Φ is invariant with respect to the shift-operators, then Φ is stationary, and
if Φ is invariant with respect to the isometries on Rd, then Φ is isotropic. From now on,
if not stated otherwise, we assume that X = Rd, and that G = {θx : x ∈Rd} is the group
of shift-operators with the natural group action on Rd. Further, we do not construct the
shift explicitly in most examples, as it is clear how to define it from context. For an
example of a proper construction; see [55, Theorem 4.3].

Second moment properties
While the intensity suffices to analyze the first-order properties of an invariant random
complex measure, we are mostly interested in second-order properties in this paper. To
this end, the reduced second moment measure is the second-order equivalent of the in-
tensity. Suppose that Φ is an invariant random complex measure on Rd which is locally
absolutely square-integrable, i.e., E[|Φ|(B1)2] < ∞, where B1 := {x ∈Rd : ∥x∥< 1}
is the open ball of radius 1 around 0. Then we can define its reduced second moment
measure by

Bartlett spectral measure
Basically, the Bartlett spectral measure allows for an analysis of the second-order proper-
ties of a random complex measure in the Fourier domain. It is named after M. S. Bartlett
since he brought it up in [2]. To approach it, we first recall the Fourier transform of a
function f ∈L1(Rd, C) as
ˆf(k) :=
Z

k ∈Rd,

(2.19)

so-called tempered distributions. However, we only need to extend it to positive semi-
definite locally finite complex measures. A locally finite complex measure µ on Rd is
called positive semi-definite if
µ(f ⋆f) ≥0

Tensor-valued random complex measures
Since we apply Taylor’s theorem to functions with d-dimensional domain in the later
sections, we not only have to work with complex-valued measures but also tensor-valued
measures. However, we limit ourselves to just handling these objects component-wise
and do not derive analogues of the reduced second moment measure, covariance measure,
and Bartlett spectral measure for the full objects, even though it would be possible; see,
e.g., [101, Section 20].
We write (Cd)⊗q, q ∈N0, for the space of rank q tensors of Cd. We equip it with the
scalar product and norm it inherits from the natural embedding into the space Cdq. The
corresponding norm is called the Frobenius norm. Note that scalars are rank 0 tensors,
vectors are rank 1 tensors, and matrices are rank 2 tensors. For two tensors x ∈(Cd)⊗q1,
y ∈(Cd)⊗q2 and q1, q2 ∈N0, we denote the tensor product by x ⊗y, which then is a rank
q1 + q2 tensor. Further, for a tensor x and q ∈N0, we write x⊗q for the q-times tensor
product of x with itself. Note that we have ∥x ⊗y∥≤∥x∥∥y∥, ∥x⊗q∥≤∥x∥q for two
tensors x, y and q ∈N0 by the submultiplicativity of the Frobenius norm. We access the
components of the tensors via indices, i.e.,
xj1,...,jq := ⟨x, ej1 ⊗· · · ⊗ejq⟩,
x ∈(Cd)⊗q, j1, ..., jq ∈{1, ..., d}, q ∈N,
(2.25)
12


## Page 13


where ej is the j-th unit vector of Rd for j ∈{1, ..., d}. Given a bounded tensor valued
function f, we simply define ∥f∥∞:= ∥g∥∞, where g is the [0, ∞)-valued concatenation
of f and the Frobenius norm.
2.2
Transport kernels
In the following sections, we analyze the effect of transports on specific second moment
properties of invariant random complex measures. These transports are handled as so-
called transport kernels. Let (X, X) and (Y, Y) be measurable spaces. Then K : X×Y →
C is called a kernel from X to Y if K(x, ·) is a complex measure for every x ∈X and
K(·, B) is measurable for every B ∈Y. If K(x, ·) has finite total variation for every
x ∈X, then we also call it a transport kernel. If K(x, ·) is a probability measure for
every x ∈X, then we also call it a probability kernel. To simplify notation, we define
|K|(x, B) := |K(x)|(B) for x ∈X, B ∈Y, and if |K|(x)(Y) is bounded independent of
x ∈X, then we say that the kernel has uniformly bounded total variation. Note that
in [55] transport kernels are always assumed to be probability kernels, which is not the
case here. Besides that, most of the following is introduced there in the same way in [65].
Now, if we suppose that ϕ is a locally finite complex measure on a metric space X,
that K is a transport kernel from X to X, and that the integral in
Kϕ(B) :=
Z
K(x, B) ϕ(dx)
(2.26)
converges for every B ∈Bb(X), then we call K a transport kernel of a transport from the
source ϕ to the destination Kϕ. If K is a probability kernel, then one can think about this
transport as taking the mass of ϕ from every location x ∈X and distributing it according
to K(x). Note that two kernels K, L can also be concatenated by
KL(x, ·) :=
Z
K(y, ·) L(x, dy),
(2.27)
and that the concatenation is associative. This concept can then be extended to the
transport of random complex measures. Note that a random complex measure Φ on a
metric space X can be interpreted as a kernel from Ωto X. A (random) transport kernel
K would then be a kernel from Ω× X to X, and if
|K||Φ|(ω, ·) :=

(2.28)
is locally finite, then
KΦ(ω, ·) :=
Z

(2.29)
is a well-defined random complex measure on X. From now onward, we also use Kx :=
K(x) := K(·, x, ·) for x ∈X to simplify the notation. Moreover, operations that act
on measures are applied ω-wise to Kx. Let us again focus on the case X = Rd. There,
such (random) transport kernels can be obtained from the disintegration of a so-called
transport, which is a random measure on Rd × Rd; see, e.g., [55, Proposition 2.2]. Else-
where, a transport is also called coupling, see, e.g., [96], or translocation; see, e.g., [50].
13


## Page 14


Alternatively, a transport kernel can be a random element of the Skorokhod space of
all c`adl`ag-functions from Rd to the (metrized) space of finite complex measures; see [55,
Subsection 5.3] for the special case that the complex measures are probability measures
and also [47]. The latter case is particularly important if one wants to assume that the
transport kernel and the source are independent.
Just like with complex random measures, we are again interested in invariance as a
form of (joint) stationarity or isotropy. A random transport kernel K from Rd to Rd is
called invariant if
K(gω, gx, gB) = K(ω, x, B)
g ∈G, ω ∈Ω, x ∈Rd, B ∈Bd,
(2.30)

to work with the kernel K∗defined by
K∗(ω, x, B) := K(ω, x, B + x), ω ∈Ω, x ∈Rd, B ∈Bd,
(2.31)
which describes the transport relative to the position x. Note that K and K∗uniquely
determine each other, and that invariance of K is equivalent to
K∗(gω, gx, B) = K∗(ω, x, B)
g ∈G, ω ∈Ω, x ∈Rd, B ∈Bd.
(2.32)
Invariance is further compatible with concatenation, i.e., if K, L are invariant transports
and if Φ is an invariant random complex measure, then LK and KΦ are also invariant.
coming to the most important case that G is the group of translations, then we also obtain
that KΦ has a finite intensity if Φ has finite intensity and K has uniformly bounded total
variation. If K is a probability kernel, then the intensity even persists under transport.

An important example for a transport kernel K is one where Kx is a Dirac measure for
every x ∈Rd. Then K is called a displacement kernel. Another possibility is a transport
where the mass is not preserved. For example, one does not move anything and simply
adds a weight, i.e., Kx = Z(x)δx with Z(x) ∈C for x ∈Rd. In this case, the transport
is associated with the C-valued random field Z on Rd. Sometimes we identify Z with
the the random complex measure Zλd := Kλd. A typical example is a GRF (Gaussian
random field), where every finite collection of random variables of the random field is
distributed according to some multivariate normal distribution. An invariant GRF Z is
14


## Page 15


called centered if its mean µ := E[Z(0)] is 0. For measurability reasons, we require Z
to lie in the Skorokhod space defined earlier. Hence, it has continuous paths, and the
covariance function C(y) := E[Z(y)Z(0)] is also continuous. On the other hand, one can
construct a centered GRF for any covariance function C which is H¨older continuous of
some positive degree. Therefore, for any finite measure ν on Rd such that k 7→∥k∥ϑ is
integrable for some ϑ > 0, one can choose Z in such a way that βZ = ν. Finally, we can
extend these C-valued random fields to Cd- or even (Cd)⊗q-valued random fields just like
we extended random complex measures to tensor-valued complex random measures. For
a vector-valued random field, the expectation is a vector and the covariance is a matrix.
We can handle a tensor-valued GRF in the same way if we consider that we can identify
(Cd)⊗q and Cdq. For more details; see, e.g., [51, Section 5].
3
Definition and properties of p-uniformity
In this section, we formalize a concept from physics, commonly referred to as the scaling
exponent (see, e.g., [89, Subsection 5.3]), by introducing p-uniformity. This framework
applies to all locally square-integrable invariant random complex measures and encom-
passes the concepts of hyperuniformity [92], the three classes of hyperuniformity [103,
89], its scaling exponent (also called hyperuniformity exponent in [68, 60]), and stealthy
hyperuniformity [3, 88].
We begin directly with the definition of p-uniformity, which relies on the Bartlett
spectral measure as in [73], where the latter is accessed through the function Z. While a
definition only utilizing real space quantities would also be possible, it is more convenient
to formulate the definition in Fourier space.
Later, in Theorem 3.7, we establish the
corresponding real-space characterization. Finally, one should note that in [78] a similar
notion of a degree of uniformity has been introduced introduced. Up to edge cases and
some conventions, our scale is essentially the same, shifted by −d. Precisely these edge
cases, however, prevent us from using their definition.
Definition 3.1. Let p ∈[−d, ∞). Suppose that Φ is a locally square-integrable invariant
random complex measure, i.e., E[|Φ(B1)|2] < ∞. Then Φ is called p-uniform if, as ε →0,
ˆβΦ(Bε)
εd
= O(εp).
(3.1)
Similarly, Φ is called beyond p-uniform if, as ε →0,
ˆβΦ(Bε)
εd
= o(εp).
(3.2)
Further, Φ is called ∞-uniform with radius ε if ˆβΦ(Bε) = 0 for some ε > 0. Note that
Bε := {x ∈Rd : ∥x∥< ε} is the open ball of radius ε around 0.
For p < ∞, if Φ is p-uniform but not beyond p-uniform, then it is called solely p-
uniform. Moreover, if Φ is ∞-uniform with radius ε but not ∞-uniform with radius ϑ for
any ϑ > ε, then it is called ∞-uniform with maximal radius ε.
Finally, a tensor-valued random complex measure is called (beyond) p-uniform (with
radius ε) if all of its components are.

hyperfluctuating, i.e., the above limit is infinite. For p > 0, p-uniformity is related to the
three classes of hyperuniformity and the so-called hyperuniformity exponent. Moreover,
∞-uniformity is equivalent to stealthy hyperuniformity. Basically, if the Bartlett spectral
measure ˆβΦ has a density with respect to the Lebesgue measure, i.e., the structure factor
SΦ is well defined, and if for some p > −d and a ≥0, as k →0,
SΦ(k) = a∥k∥p + o(∥k∥p),
(3.4)
then Φ is p-uniform. Further, Φ is also beyond p-uniform iff a = 0. Details and proofs
follow after we establish some basic properties. To get a better grasp on the concept
of p-uniformity, one can also take a look at the Examples 3.22, where p-uniform point
processes are given for various p ∈[−d, ∞].
Remark 3.2. The concept of p-uniformity admits an order, and p-uniformity for higher
p always implies p-uniformity for lower p in the following sense. Let p, q ∈[−d, ∞] and
ε, ϑ > 0. Suppose that Φ is a locally square-integrable invariant random complex measure.
• If Φ is beyond p-uniform, then Φ is also p-uniform.
• If Φ is p-uniform and p > q, then Φ is also beyond q-uniform.
• If Φ is ∞-uniform with radius ε and ε ≥ϑ, then Φ is also ∞-uniform with radius ϑ.
This order also implies that if Φ is solely p-uniform, then Φ is q-uniform iff q ≤p.
Moreover, if Φ is ∞-uniform with maximal degree ε, then Φ is ∞-uniform with degree ϑ
iff ϑ ≤ε.
Further, one may wonder why p-uniformity is defined only for p ≥−d. The reason
is that (−d)-uniformity is already trivial in the sense that every locally square-integrable
invariant random complex measure is (−d)-uniform. By the ordered nature of the concept,
p-uniformity remains trivial for p < −d.
Still, the following remark gives an insight
into beyond (−d)-uniformity, which turns out to be directly related to pseudo-ergodicity.
A locally square-integrable invariant random complex measure is called pseudo-ergodic

Having treated the extreme case of (−d)-uniformity, we can come to the real-space
characterization of p-uniformity for p ∈(−d, ∞). The case p = ∞is treated in Theo-
rem 3.15. The connection between real space and Fourier space in the context of hyper-
uniformity was already explored in [92], where hyperuniformity itself was first introduced.
As seen in the following theorem, the characterization involves a test function. In these
early versions, the test function was assumed to be an indicator function, in particular
the indicator function of the unit ball. In this case, the relation only holds one-to-one for
p-uniformity with p ∈(0, 1), which is also called class III hyperuniformity; see [89, Sub-
section 5.3]. For other indicator functions, the relation can break down even earlier; see,
e.g., [54]. More recently, a criterion for the decay of the Fourier transform of the indicator
function was introduced, which guarantees that the characterization of p-uniformity with
respect to this indicator function works up to a certain p; see [8, Theorem 3.7]. Still, the
characterization was limited to p < 1, which comes from the fact that, at this point, only
indicator functions were considered. The following theorem generalizes the connection
between real space and Fourier space with respect to p-uniformity, building upon their
proof. For higher degrees, smoother test functions are needed, but for lower degrees,
indicator functions of nicely behaved sets are still sufficient. Similar generalizations were
made in [7, 68, 59, 6, 60]. It should also be noted that much earlier, while not explicitly
stated, these techniques were already utilized in [33]. Our proof is inspired by [8, Theorem
3.6 and 3.7] and requires us to make the following extension to a definition of theirs.

we can use it to rigorously establish the connection between our spectral definition of

Going further, we can relate our notion of p-uniformity to the scaling exponent of the
structure factor close to 0 that is typically considered by the physics community; see,
e.g., [89, Subsection 5.3]. In the mathematics community, this scaling exponent has also
been called the hyperuniformity exponent if it is positive; see [68, 60].

Further, we can connect p-uniformity to the three classes of hyperuniformity; see [103]
and [89, Subsection 5.3]. In Theorem 3.7, one assumes that the test function is always
smooth enough with respect to p. However, if the test function is not sufficiently smooth,
the decay of the variance is limited even if the random complex measure is p-uniform for
very hight p, as it can be seen in the following theorem. If one considers the test function
22


## Page 23


f = 1B1, one obtains the three classes of uniformity: class III, when the p-uniformity of
Φ is the limiting factor for the decay of the variance, i.e., if Φ is solely p-uniform for some
p ∈(0, 1); class I, when the smoothness of 1B1 is the limiting factor, i.e., if Φ is p-uniform
for some p > 1; and class II, where both factors (can) contribute at the transition. One
can find similar results for the upper bound in the survey [60], and in the special case
that f = 1B1 in [89, 17]. In the special case that p = 1 and that Φ is a point process, the
lower bound we give was first proven in [4] using an alternative method. More precisely,
the author derived a stronger version of the lower bound, which does not hold in our more
general setting.

Having covered the real-space implications of p-uniformity for p < ∞, let us come
to a real-space characterization of ∞-uniformity as an analogue to Theorem 3.7. The
argument comes from [33].
Theorem 3.15. Suppose that Φ is a locally absolutely square-integrable invariant random
complex measure. Then Φ is ∞-uniform iff there is a function f ∈L1(Rd, C) with λd(f) ̸=
0 such that Var[Φ(f)] = 0. Further, if ˆf(k) ̸= 0 for all k ∈Bε and some ε > 0, then
Φ is ∞-uniform with radius ε. Conversely, if Φ is ∞-uniform with radius ε > 0, then
Var[Φ(f)] = 0 for all f ∈L1(Rd, C) with ˆf(k) = 0 for all k ∈Bc
ε.

for every p ∈N0, then it is also maximally rigid by a simple density argument. The
foundational work that connects hyperuniformity and rigidity can again be found in [32,
33], on which the more recent and general work [61] builds upon. Our contribution in the
following proposition is to show that (2p + d)-uniformity implies the sufficient conditions
for p-rigidity from [61]. There, this connection was only established in special cases.
Proposition 3.17. Let p ∈N0. Suppose that Φ is a locally absolutely square-integrable
invariant random complex measure. If Φ is (2p+d)-uniform, then Φ is p-rigid. Further, if
Φ is q-uniform for all q ∈[−d, ∞), in particular, if Φ is ∞-uniform, then Φ is maximally
rigid.

For the characterization of p-uniformity in Theorem 3.7 to hold, it was

We close this section by giving p-uniform examples for different p ∈[−d, ∞]. This
question is trivial when it comes to random signed measures, as a Gaussian random field
that is solely p-uniform can be constructed easily for any p ∈[−d, ∞]. Therefore, all
given examples are nonnegative random measures and, in particular, even simple point
processes.
Examples 3.22. The following examples are ordered from p-uniform with low p to high
p. All but the first are ergodic.
(a) Suppose that N is a nondeterministic, nonnegative random variable such that
E[N −2d] < ∞. Further, suppose that U ∼U([0, 1)d) is independent of N. Then
the randomly scaled stationary lattice P
z∈Zd δN(z+U) is solely (−d)-uniform. This
is a simple consequence of Proposition 3.3 and the fact that the spatial mean of the
point process is N −d.
(b) A stationary Poisson hyperplane intersection process is solely (−d+1)-uniform. This
fact can be simply derived from Theorem 3.7 and the asymptotics of the variance
derived in [38]; see also [57].
(c) A stationary Poisson process is solely 0-uniform, which is a direct consequence of
the fact that the Bartlett spectral measure is a multiple of the Lebesgue measure in
this case.
(d) The Ginibre point process is solely 2-uniform. The same holds for many other hy-
peruniform determinantal point processes, and in general, nontrivial determinantal
point processes cannot be beyond 2-uniform; see, e.g., [60, Subsection 3.3].
(e) The zeros of the planar Gaussian analytic function are solely 4-uniform; see [84].
(f) The stationary lattice Zd+U with U ∼U([0, 1)d) is ∞-uniform with maximal radius
2π. This can be directly derived from the fact that it has the Bartlett spectral
measure P
z∈Zd\{0} δ2πz, which is a consequence of the Poisson summation formula.
This list leaves large gaps on the scale without proper examples. On the left end of
the scale, we construct ergodic solely p-uniform point processes with an arbitrarily low
p < 0 in Appendix D. However, our main interest lies in the construction of ergodic
point processes which fill the gap between the degree of 4 and ∞. A construction given
in [61] which was proven to result in a solely 2p-uniform point process for any p that is
30


## Page 31


prime for d = 2. This construction can be artificially extended to higher dimensions. In
contrast, see Subsection 5.2. There, we explore a wider class of point processes which
can be solely 2p-uniform for any p ∈N natively in any dimension. Moreover, since the
construction in [61] is a subclass of the class explored in Subsection 5.2, we also prove
that the restriction to p prime imposed there is not necessary. While the examples in
Subsection 5.2 are a natural derivation from the results of the following Section 4, they
are also strongly connected to an idea from [28]. Another class of point processes, that
can be p-uniform for arbitrarily high p, and that is arguably larger and more relevant
in applications, is explored in Subsection 5.1. The trade-off is that, when we obtain p-
uniformity in the latter class of point processes, we usually cannot expect the process to
be solely p-uniform. All of these examples are derived from the theory we build in the
following section on the effect of transportation on p-uniformity.
4
On p-uniformity under transports
The effect of transportation on p-uniformity can only be studies if both the source and
destination have a locally finite second moment. While it is easy to see that a probability
kernel preserves a locally finite first moment, the same does not hold for the second
moment.
Further complications arise if the transport kernel is not assumed to be a
probability kernel.
In the following subsection, we derive sufficient conditions for the
preservation of a locally finite second moment under transportation. It turns out that
these conditions even lay a foundation for the results on the preservation of p-uniformity
in the subsections thereafter.
4.1
Persistence of square integrability under transport
There is no single function that makes the central Condition 4.2 for our first Theorem 4.4
sharp. However, the following class of functions does.
Definition 4.1 (Strongly log-dominating). Let ρ : [0, ∞) →[0, ∞) be an increasing
function that satisfies
Z ∞
0
1
rρ(r) + 1 dr < ∞.
(4.1)
Then ρ is called a strongly log-dominating function.
As suggested by the name, ρ is not a strongly log-dominating function if ρ(r) =
log(r + 1), r ≥0. However, many other slowly increasing functions are admissible. For
instance, ρ is strongly log-dominating if ρ(r) = rϑ or even ρ(r) = log(r + 1)1+ϑ, r ≥0, for
some ϑ > 0.
In the following, we work with two different integrability conditions. One is for the
more general case, where the transport kernel K can be non-mass-preserving and depen-
dent on the random measure Φ it transports. The other is specifically adapted to the case
that K is a probability kernel (or of uniformly bounded total variation) and independent
of Φ. Both conditions can be considered sharp within their respective context, as it can be
seen in Remark 4.19 and 4.20. If not stated otherwise, we assume that K is an invariant

Still, these integrability conditions alone do not suffice to preserve p-uniformity if
d + p > 2. In the special cases that d = 2 with p > 0 or d ≥3 with p = 0, this fact was
already discovered in [21]. We close this gap by introducing a Taylor expansion of the
transport; see Theorem 4.14. The terms of this expansion contain the random complex
measures Ψq defined in (4.15). In the following theorem, we show how conditions on
these can ensure the persistence of p-uniformity for any p ∈[−d, ∞] and in any dimension
d ∈N. Note that we also treat the case that K and Φ are dependent, which has not been
considered before to the best of our knowledge.
Theorem 4.13. Suppose that Φ is a locally absolutely square-integrable invariant random

The following Theorem 4.14 introduces the Taylor expansion of the transport and lays
the foundation of the proof of the preceding Theorem 4.13. It is also of independent
interest, as it allows for a more detailed analysis of the (asymptotic) behavior of the
destination of the transport. The strongest version assumes Condition 4.12 and allows for
representing the transport as a Taylor series. It would imply all of the preceding results
if one were satisfied with the assumption of a finite exponential moment of the typical
transport distance. However, under the weaker assumption of Condition 4.10 or 4.11,
one can still control the error of the finite expansion, which suffices for the analysis of
p-uniformity for p < ∞. Recall Definition 3.5.

Under the conditions of the preceding theorem, one can directly analyze the asymptotic
behavior of KΦ by relating it to the leading order term of the approximation, which comes
from some Ψq0. For q0 = 0, in particular when p ≤−d + 2, one can even derive that KΦ
is (beyond) p-uniform iff Ψq0 is (beyond) p-uniform. However, for higher q0, derivatives
get involved. Since derivatives always integrate to 0, there can be some cancellation such
that KΦ actually is ˜p-uniform for a higher ˜p than Ψq0. Additionally, if d ≥2, Ψq0 is
tensor-valued and the components can be correlated such that the combined variance on
the RHS of (4.33) is of lower order than the variances of the individual components of
Ψq0. However, if d = 1, and if Ψq0 is (pseudo-) ergodic, then Proposition 3.19 yields
that, for every p ∈(−d, ∞), KΦ is (beyond) p-uniform iff Ψq0 is (beyond p-uniform. For
∞-uniformity, the same problems apply when q0 ≥1 and d ≥2, but still, if q0 = 0, or
43


## Page 44


if d = 1 and Ψq0 is (pseudo-) ergodic, then KΦ is ∞-uniform with radius ε0 iff Ψq0 is
∞-uniform with radius ε0.
Having finished the presentation of the central results of this paper, we proceed with
giving two approaches for checking the assumptions of these theorems. We do not elabo-
rate on the Conditions 4.10 and 4.12, as the simplifications are analogous to those made in
Proposition 4.5 and Remark 4.6. The first approach for checking the remaining assump-
tions works under a certain degree of decorrelation and is only applicable to p-uniformity
with p ≤2. It can be compared to an approach in [55], where a certain kind of β-mixing
was assumed. None of the approaches is strictly stronger than the other, but the approach
here yields p-uniformity and not only hyperuniformity, and while we have an additional
moment condition here, the decorrelation assumption strictly is weaker, which can be seen
in Proposition 5.27. There, it is also shown that our condition is strictly weaker than the
one used in [26].

As is the case with independent and identically distributed (iid) perturbations (see
Example 5.15), spatial decorrelation of the transport alone does not preserve p-uniformity
for p > 2. The following approach uses a different technique to circumvent this limitation.
While it is a lot more restrictive that just decorrelation, it allows for the construction of
many interesting examples in Subsections 5.1 and 5.2.

In Subsection 5.1, we appliy this comparison approach to a non-deterministic L which

we choose a deterministic L. There, the conditions are even improved by combining the
approach from Proposition 4.18 with the decorrelation approach from Proposition 4.16.
We conclude this subsection by showing that the Conditions 4.10 and 4.11 we assume
in this subsection and, in particular, the main Theorem 4.13 are basically sharp.
To
simplify we only consider the case p ≤0, i.e., show that Conditions 4.2 and 4.3 are sharp.
For the more general case, one needs to replace the power of d by d + p in some places.
In the first remark, we show that the strongly log-dominating function ρ is necessary
for Condition 4.2 to be sufficient in Theorem 4.4 and that it cannot be dropped like in
the more specialized Condition 4.3.

4.3
Persistence of p-uniformity under spatial interpolation
It is clear that the assumptions made in Theorem 4.13 are sufficient but not necessary.
However, if one restricts the class of transports a little further, the same assumptions
guarantee that the transport K of the source Φ also fulfills a much stronger property
regarding a spatial inter- and even extrapolation of the source Φ and destination KΦ. In
this setting, the assumptions come a lot closer to being necessary.
Here, we always assume that Φ is a locally square-integrable invariant complex random
measure, and that K is an invariant probability kernel. Further, we assume that almost
surely, for Φ-almost all x ∈Rd, Kx has bounded support, i.e.,
P(Φ({x ∈Rd : diam(supp(Kx)) = ∞) = 0) = 1.
(4.51)

In the special case that d = 1 and that K and Φ are ergodic, these implications are
actually equivalent to the (p −2q)-uniformity assumptions made in Theorem 4.13; see
Proposition 3.19. For higher dimensions, the picture is more complicated. In conclusion,
the assumptions made in Theorem 4.13 seem to almost be necessary for a transport to
preserve p-uniformity from the source to the destination and along the way. For an even
closer relation in both directions for d ≥2, one probably needs to more directly analyze
the second-moment properties of invariant tensor-valued random complex measures.
5
Applications
5.1
Point processes from invariant partitions
In this subsection, we apply our theorems to random partitions of space, We thus obtain
point processes that are p-uniform for arbitrarily high p. We begin by formally defining
what we mean by a random partition of space.
Definition 5.1. Suppose that Φ is an invariant locally square-integrable simple point
process and suppose that τ is an invariant allocation. We then call (Φ, τ) an invariant
random partition if τ(x) ∈Φ for all x ∈Rd. Further, for x ∈Φ, we call C(x) := τ −1(x) a
cell of (Φ, τ). Finally, V0 := C(τ(0)) is called the zero-cell.
Additionally, if λd(C(x)) = γ−1 for all x ∈Φ, where γ is the intensity of Φ, then we
call (Φ, τ) a random fair partition.
The underlying principle for the following theorem, from which all examples in this
subsection are derived, was already discovered in physics in [28]. Our contribution is
that we can apply our results from Section 4 to control the effect of correlations in the
partition. Therefore, we can formulate the statement rigorously and in greater generality,
which allows for applications that were probably not in view when the idea was first
stated.
Theorem 5.2. Let p ∈[−d, ∞). Suppose that (Φ, τ) is an invariant random partition,
and suppose that Ψ, Γ are invariant locally square-integrable random measures. Further,
assume that, for some ϑ > 0,

In the following, we construct point processes which are (beyond) p-uniform, where
a high p is of particular interest.
Practically, we start with a random fair partition.
Then we construct a point process by placing an equal number of points in each cell
such that they satisfy the moment condition (5.2), where Ψ is the point process and
Γ is a multiple of λd. This condition is equivalent to the points, together with equal
weights, forming a quadrature formula of degree max(⌈d+p
2 ⌉−1, 0) for their respective
cell. Hence, the existence of such quadrature formulae is necessary to construct these
point processes. While the existence of regular quadrature formulas can be easily derived
from Carath´eodory’s theorem, the restriction to equal-weight quadrature formulas, which
are also called averaging sets, makes the problem a lot harder. Still, the construction of
averaging sets of rank 0 or 1 (or even 2) is always possible, which gives rise to the two
following examples. The first allows for the arbitrary placement of one point per cell.
These methods are also applicable to non-fair random partitions if one passes down
the volume of the cells to the mass placed within; see also [52, 55]. However, one thus
obtains a random measure and generally not a point process.

Example 5.6. The pinwheel tiling is a fair partition of R2. If we apply Example 5.4,
we can see that the arbitrary placement of one point in each cell results in a beyond
0-uniform, i.e., hyperuniform, point process. This result supports the findings in [82].
Moreover, if the points are each placed independently and uniformly in the cells like
in [27], we can even combine techniques with the hyperuniformerer from [55, Example
4.7] to obtain that the constructed point process is solely 2-uniform, in particular, class
I hyperuniform. Finally, the placement in the centroids even yields a beyond 2-uniform
point process by Example 5.5. The maximal p for which the centroids are p-uniform has
not been found yet.
To guarantee that the procedure produces point processes which are beyond 0-uniform
in any dimension or to guarantee p-uniformity for higher p, one needs more points per
cell. Proposition D.7 shows that, even in a random fair partition with highly regular cells,
the centroids can form a point process which is not 0-uniform for d ≥5. This extension
52


## Page 53


necessitates a slightly higher degree of regularity on the side of the random fair partition
to guarantee the existence of a point process which fits the moment condition (5.2). In
particular, we require the volume of the convex hull of the cells to be uniformly bounded.
If the cells of a fair partition are convex, they immediately satisfy this condition, and
as λd(conv(V0)) ≤κd diam(V0)d, cells with uniformly bounded diameter also suffice. In
Proposition C.12, it can be seen why a restriction of this kind is necessary.

Now that we have shown how to construct p-uniform point processes with an arbitrarily
high p from invariant random fair partitions, we shift our focus to the construction of the
partitions themselves. One class of invariant fair partitions is obtained from lattices and
their corresponding lattice tilings. We follow an approach closely connected to this one
in Subsection 5.2. However, lattice tilings are not isotropic, which is another property
we are interested in. If one artificially makes them isotropic by randomly rotating them,
they are no longer ergodic, which we also want them to be. Another class consists of
fair tilings that can be generated from matching algorithms. This class actually contains
isotropic and ergodic examples. A discussion of multiple options can be found in [55].
However, the classical stable matching rarely yields fair tilings which fulfill the moment
condition (5.13); see [40]. While there are alternatives like gravitational allocation for
d = 3 [16], which are regular enough for Example 5.4 and 5.5, their cell shapes may not
53


## Page 54


satisfy the stricter assumptions of Example 5.7. Finally, complete matchings are usually
hard to simulate at a truly large scale, since most of the corresponding algorithms exhibit
superlinear complexity.
In the following, we introduce such an invariant, isotropic, and ergodic random fair
partition with convex cells in any dimension that has a finite approximation that can
be simulated in linear time. However, a rigorous proof for the existence is beyond the
scope of this paper, as it requires distinct techniques, and in our opinion, deserves its own
proper investigation.
Our method is closely connected to the so-called STIT tessellations (stable with re-
spect to iterations).
These arise from Markov chains on the space of partitions with
convex cells, where in every step the cells are randomly and independently divided by
hyperplanes and then scaled up to preserve the scale of the original partition. See [72,
71] for further details. Our construction differs in that the Markov chain is constructed
on the space of fair partitions with convex cells, i.e., in every step every cell is split ex-
actly once into two parts of equal volume. We can also allow for the distribution of the
split direction to depend on the cell shape, which was not allowed in this freedom in the
original STIT models. Although the models are not a subclass of the STIT models and
more akin to a variant of the underlying idea, we call these models the fair STIT models
to emphasize their similarity.

In practice, we can just start with a fair partition and simulate multiple steps of the
Markov chain and assume that the result is, in distribution, close to a supposed limit
distribution. This approach has the additional advantage that it is easy to simulate at a
large scale. As every cell is divided independently, the number of operations we need to

in linear time. Additionally, the algorithm can be parallelized perfectly.
5.2
Point processes from moment-preserving clusters
The following constructions of point processes are also based on placing clusters of points,
but no fair partitions are involved here. Instead, we base the construction on a progenitor
point process which is ˜p-uniform for a very high ˜p, e.g., ˜p = ∞. We then decrease ˜p
by a controlled amount. While the previous construction yields nicer point processes for
practical purposes, we can also derive sole p-uniformity here.

The following theorem is analogous to the previous theorem but is concerned with
∞-uniformity.
Theorem 5.13. Let µ be a random finite measure on Rd. Suppose that Φ is a locally
square-integrable invariant point process that is ∞-uniform with radius ε > 0, and that K
is a transport kernel such that (K∗

With this theorem, we can construct the following interesting example.
Example 5.14. Suppose that Φ is a locally square-integrable invariant point process
which is ∞-uniform with radius ε > 0. Choose f : Rd →(0, ∞) such that ˆf has support on
Bϑ with ϑ ∈(0, ε). Further, let (Φx)x∈Φ, conditioned on Φ, be a sequence of independent

is a locally square-integrable invariant discrete random measure which is ∞-uniform with
radius ε −ϑ and has atoms which are everywhere dense.
In particular, if Φ is the invariant lattice, this construction results in a discrete random
measure, where in each cell of the original lattice, the locations of the new atoms are
independent and uniformly distributed over the cell.
However, for different cells the
weights are highly correlated, which guarantees that ∞-uniform is preserved.
By convoluting this discrete random measure with a suitable function, one can also
construct an invariant random measure which is ∞-uniform while having a (smooth) den-
sity with respect to the Lebesgue measure. Another variant can be obtained by replacing
the invariant lattice by the ∞-uniform point process constructed in [74].
Let us return from this small excursion about discrete random measures and nonnega-

point processes with high p. Theorem 5.12 gives us a clear direction and the next step
is to find a suitable µ that is is of the form µ = Pn

The argument is similar to those in the preceding examples, but instead of Theo-

However, the measure on the surface of the unit ball, which is approximated in the
previous example, is not uniform for d ≥3, whereby the constructed point process cannot
be isotropic in this case. Averaging sets for the uniform distribution are called spherical
designs in the literature; see, e.g., [81]. It is known that spherical designs exist in any di-
mension d ∈N, and of any degree p ∈N0, and that the minimum number of points needed
grows like pd−1 as p →∞; see [10]. In this sense, they are as efficient as the trigonometric
parametrizations from Example 5.19 with the added advantage that their class is rotation
invariant. There also exist explicit constructions of spherical designs of arbitrarily high
degree (see, e.g., [100]), but the known constructions are not asymptotically efficient in
terms of size in relation to degree for dimensions d ≥3.

5.3
Persistence of p-uniformity under displacements
In this subsection, we analyze the effect of the application of a displacement field on p-
uniformity of a random measure. Displacement fields are Rd-valued random fields on Rd.
Sometimes they are the product of a disintegration of a transport. Otherwise, in particular
when we suppose that the displacement field and the source are independent, we only
consider random fields with c`adl`ag-paths for measurability reasons. As the random fields
are multivariate, we reserve the index for the components of the random field, and we
access the individual random vectors only using the argument. We refer to Subsection 2.1
for a precise introduction.
In the following, unless explicitly stated otherwise, we always assume that Z is an in-
variant Rd-valued random field on Rd, and that Φ is an invariant locally square-integrable
random measure. We then define the invariant random measure Ψ by
Ψ(B) :=
Z

If κ(y) = 0, the bound is trivial. It remains to apply Proposition 5.25 to obtain the main
part of the assertion. Note that the moment condition (5.57) follows from (5.69).
For p = 0 and with independence between Z and Φ, similar results have been derived
before. However, our assumptions are basically the weakest. In [26, Corollary 1], even
if the perturbations are uniformly bounded, it is required that κ(y) decays like ∥y∥−d−ϑ
as y →∞for some ϑ > 0. Here we only require a decay like ∥y∥−d+2−ϑ with some
specific ϑ ∈(0, 2) depending on γ. This fact makes our result also more efficient than
the one obtained in [55, Theorem 5.1] up to the basic moment condition (5.72). We can
leverage this increased efficiency to obtain p-uniformity for some p ∈(0, 2] under the same
assumptions that would simply yield beyond 0-uniformity applying the previously derived
theorems.
For a Gaussian displacement field independent of the source, the criterion from Propo-
sition 5.25 can be simplified differently to a condition on the decay of only the first-order
correlations. Again, for the case p = 0, similar conditions have been derived before, but
the following are the most general. In [26, Corollary 2], it is required that the decay of
the bound on the RHS of (5.76) is smaller by a factor of ∥y∥−(d+2). This rate was then
improved in [55, Example 5.2], but still the rate required there is faster than the rate
required here by a factor of ∥y∥−2. The result we provide is basically sharp in the sense
that for each p ∈[−d, 2) there is a GRF (Gaussian random field) Z such that Ψ (with

Let us now come back to the Gaussian case. Then we can construct examples such that
Ψ is 4-uniform. However, at least if d = 1, we can rigorously show that it is impossible
that Ψ is beyond 4-uniform under very mild assumptions. In the physics literature, it
was already established in [29] that 4-uniformity is usually the limit in this case, but the
precise conditions were not derived yet.
Proposition 5.33. Let d = 1. Suppose that Z is a Gaussian random field, and define
C : R →[0, ∞), y 7→Cov[Z(0), Z(y)].
Assume that Φ ̸= 0, and that Z and Φ are
independent. Further, assume that ˆC is not purely discrete, which, e.g., is the case if Z is
ergodic and nondeterministic, or if lim supx→∞|C(x)| < C(0). Then Z2 · Φ is not beyond
0-uniform and Ψ is not beyond 4-uniform.
More precisely, if Z is ergodic, Φ is solely p0-uniform, Z · Φ solely p1-uniform, and
Z2 · Φ solely p2-uniform, then Ψ is solely min(p0, p1 + 2, p2 + 4)-uniform. We always have
p2 ≥0, and most of the time also p1 ≥0; see Proposition 5.32. However, if, e.g., Φ = λ1
or Φ = Z + U, U ∼U([0, 1)), then p1 < 0 is possible, and we can even choose Z such that
p1 exactly matches any value below 0 while keeping p2 = 0. In this way, p-uniformity for
any p ∈[−1, 4] is obtainable exactly with an appropriate choice of Z and Φ.

and ˜Z2 is pseudo-ergodic, whereby Proposition 3.19 applies.
Now, we can come to the last part of the proof, where we assume that Z is ergodic,
that Φ is solely p0-uniform, Z ·Φ solely p1-uniform, and Z2 ·Φ solely p2-uniform. The fact
that Ψ is min(p0, p1 + 2, p2 + 4)-uniform directly follows from (5.84) and Remark 3.6. It
remains to show that Ψ is not beyond min(p0, p1 + 2, p2 + 4)-uniform. However, this fact
also follows from (5.84). If p0 = −1, it is clear that Ψ is also solely −1-uniform, and if
p0 > −1, then Ψ is pseudo-ergodic. This property also transfers to Z · Φ and Z2 · Φ by
Proposition 5.31, as Z is assumed to be ergodic, whereby ˆC and ˆC ∗ˆC do not have any
atoms, and as Cov[Z(0)2, Z(y)2] = 2C(y)2 + 4E[Z(0)]2C(y) by Isserlis formula; see [46].
Hence, the assertion follows with Proposition 3.19.
The last two propositions suggest that Theorem 5.22, at least in the independent case,
is only applicable up to 4-uniformity in most cases. While we were able to construct
many examples for the more general transport kernels in subsections 5.1 and 5.2, we are
not aware of any interesting examples such that Zq · λd is p-uniform for high p for all
q ∈{0, ..., n} and n ≥2.
5.4
Lattices at positive temperature
As it has been shown in both physicists (see, e.g., [53, Section V]) and mathematics
(see [79, 20]), compressible particle systems are not hyperuniform, i.e., beyond 0-uniform,
at positive temperature. Physicists often model crystals at positive temperature as a
lattices with displacements, known as phonons. Here, we extended the results from a
mathematical perspective.
We consider a point process and view the positive temperature state as a random
displacement of some zero-temperature state. If these displacements were independent of
the initial state and each other, like it is assumed in the so-called Einstein model [23],
the temperature could only affect 2-uniformity; see Example 5.15. However, in the newer
Debye model [18], the displacements are allowed to be correlated, which changes the
picture.
More precisely, we consider a model from [53, Section V], where the lattice is modeled
as a point process with springs along the lattice edges.
There, it was already shown
that this system, unlike the perfect lattice, is not beyond 0-uniform. However, we add
our results to their technique to make the proof mathematically tight. To this end, we
consider only the case d ≥3, as in lower dimensions one can no longer model a thermalized
69


## Page 70


crystal as a stationarily displaced perfect crystal with a typical displacement Z that
satisfies E[∥Z(0)∥d] < ∞; see, e.g., [63, Section 137]. This integrability is a fundamental
assumption to make our techniques applicable; see Condition 4.3. On the other hand, the
techniques used here should apply not only to the lattice model we consider but also to
other Debye solids for d ≥3. In this context, the central assumption is that the dispersion
relation is linear in 0 asymptotically.
Coming back to the example at hand, in [53, Section V] it was derived that if Φ :=
Zd + U, U ∼U([0, 1)d), is the stationary lattice, the transport to its positive temperature
state can be facilitated by a transport kernel K of the form
K∗(x) := δZ(⌊x−U⌋),

## Page 72


6
Prospects
In this paper, we have focused on conditions under which p-uniformity is preserved under
transport, and have then applied these results to answer open questions. A further ques-
tion is whether p-uniform random measures are close to the perfectly uniform Lebesgue
measure in terms of some transport cost. If one considers a transport as having low cost
whenever it fulfills the conditions of Theorem 4.13, then the question is trivial by the
following proposition.
Proposition 6.1. Let p ∈[−d, ∞]. Suppose that Φ is an invariant locally absolutely
square-integrable random complex measure which is (beyond) p-uniform. Then there exists
an invariant transport kernel K such that Φ = Kλd, and such that K and λd satisfy the
conditions of Theorem 4.13.
Proof. Such a transport kernel K is explicitly constructed in Appendix A.2. There, it is
named L, and we also do so in this proof. As in (A.12), we choose it as

of Ψq for q ∈N0 as defined in (4.15). Let q ∈N0 and suppose that q1, ..., qd ∈{0, ..., q}
such that q1 + · · · + qd = q. Then,

Therefore, Theorem 4.13 and Proposition 6.1 together yield a characterization of p-
uniformity in terms of an invariant transport with source λd.
Theorem 6.2. Let Φ be a random complex measure. Then Φ is invariant, locally ab-
solutely square-integrable, and (beyond) p-uniform iff there exists an invariant transport
kernel K such that Φ = Kλd, and such that K and λd satisfy the conditions of Theo-
rem 4.13.
This characterization of p-uniformity shows that the conditions in Theorem 4.13 are
well-chosen. Still, it is not that powerful since the transport constructed in Proposition 6.1
is not mass-preserving and does not allow for the construction of a spatial interpolation
72


## Page 73


like in Subsection 4.3. If, instead, we restrict the kernel K to be a probability kernel,
then our moment conditions on the transport are closely connected to the Wasserstein
distance; see Definition 4.7. In [62, 14, 43], it was shown that for d = 2, up to edge cases,
all hyperuniform random measures have a finite 2-Wasserstein distance to the Lebesgue
measure. However, for d ̸= 2 the picture is different. For the case d = 1, see [24], and
for d ≥3, not even finite ∞-Wasserstein distance to the Lebesgue measure is close to
implying hyperuniformity; see [21, Theorem 1] or Proposition D.6. For this reason, we
propose the following distance to (almost) characterize p-uniformity. It involves strong
restrictions on higher-order moments if the parameter is large. We restrict ourselves to
the distance to the Lebesgue measure because the sharpness of Proposition 4.9 shows
that only very regular invariant random measures are compatible with a condition on a
Wasserstein-type distance.

Conjecture 6.4. Let p ≥−d+2. Suppose that Φ is an invariant locally absolutely square-
integrable random (nonnegative) measure with intensity γ. Then dd+p(Φ, γλd) < ∞if Φ
is (p + ϑ)-uniform for some ϑ > 0.
Compared to the preceding Proposition 6.1, this conjecture is no complete inversion
of Theorem 4.13, as dd+p(Φ, γλd) < ∞only implies that Φ is beyond (2⌊d+p

for p ≥0, and for p < 0 not even local square integrability of Φ is guaranteed. However,
a complete inversion is actually not possible under these restrictions.
Let us now motivate this conjecture. For d = 1, it was shown in [24] that p-uniformity
does not suffice to conclude that dd+p(Φ, γλd) < ∞for p < 1. On the other hand, in [14]
it was shown that (−d + 2 + ϑ)-uniformity implies d2(Φ, γλd) < ∞for d ≥2, ϑ > 0. For
d = 1, their bound involves a logarithmic term that one might be able to avoid via a
smoother test function, which would extend the applicability of their result to d = 1. For
73

are no results available in the literature so far, since a distance of our type, to the best
of our knowledge, has not been considered yet, and a classical Wasserstein distance no
longer suffices at that point, as discussed above. Still, we can prove a weaker version of
the conjecture, where we assume that Φ is not only p-uniform for some p < ∞but also
∞-uniform.

In particular, we obtain that every invariant locally absolutely square-integrable ran-
dom measure which is ∞-uniform has a finite p-Wasserstein distance to the Lebesgue
measure for any p ≥1. If the random measure is a point process, we can derive from
this fact that it is an Lp-perturbed lattice for any p ≥1; see [42]. However, at least for
d ≥3, this fact is not surprising, since even the Poisson process exhibits this property
there; see [44].
Analogous transports can also be constructed if one replaces γλd by the invariant
lattice γ−1(Zd + U) with U ∼U([0, 1)d), where γ−1 is not a density but a stretching
factor. In this case, we can choose L to depend only on U.

A.2
Reduction to Lebesgue
In this subsection, we show why one can sometimes assume that the source of the transport
is λd without loss of generality. This fact is of particular importance if the transport
kernel and the source are allowed to be dependent, as the dependence drops out with this
simplification. In the following, we always suppose that Φ is a locally square-integrable
invariant random complex measure and that K is an invariant transport kernel. Further,
we define the random transport kernel L by

A.3
Square integrability
The main focus of this subsection is to bound a certain remainder term which comes up
if one applies the Taylor approximation from Lemma A.1. However, we first show a small
lemma that allows us to assume that strongly log-dominating functions do not grow too
quickly, which makes sense, as they are supposed to be slowly increasing functions.

Fourier-smoothness
We recall that by Definition 3.4 a function f ∈L1(Rd, C) is called Fourier-smooth with
exponent p ∈[−d, ∞) if there is a c > 0 such that
| ˆf(k)| ≤c
1

Note that (B.2) follows directly from (B.1) if p > 0.
This definition is an extension
of the definition from [8], where it was introduced for indicator functions with exponents
limited to (0, 1]. While the extension to non-indicator functions is trivial, the non-positive
exponents require the additional assumption of (B.2) to make sure that the assertions of
Theorem 3.7 hold also in this case. There may be a more optimal formulation of the
additional condition, but in the following, we see that ours at least allows for the correct
classification of the important 1[0,1)d.
Examples B.1. The indicator function of the unit ball 1B1 is Fourier-smooth with ex-
ponent 1 and not any higher exponent; see [8, Remark 3.5] and [45, Theorem 2.16]. The
indicator function of the unit cube 1[0,1)d is Fourier-smooth with exponent −d + 2 and
not any higher exponent by Lemma B.2. Hence, the latter cannot be used to reliably
detect hyperuniformity in general; see Theorem 3.7 and also [54]. Further, Schwartz func-
tions are Fourier-smooth with any exponent. In particular, these include the functions in
C∞
c (Rd, C).

that covers all functions that are the product of one-dimensional functions.

C
Averaging sets
Averaging sets are a special class of quadrature formula where the weights are all equal.
The term was coined in [81] as a generalization of so-called spherical t-designs, which
are averaging sets for the uniform surface integral on a sphere; see also [19, 10]. Our
main contribution here lies in Appendix C.1, where we show that for fixed dimension and
degree, there is a size such that there exist averaging sets of that size of all convex sets; see
Definition C.6 for the properties of such an averaging set. This fact is relevant to ensure
the existence of the point processes constructed in Subsection 5.1. Then in Appendix C.2,
we generalize Chebyshev–Gauss quadrature to approximate image measures of arbitrary
trigonometric polynomials. This technique is applied in Subsection 5.2. There, we also
use spherical formulas, but the relevant results about them have already been provided
in [10], so we do not explicitly introduce them here.

Before we continue, we make a quick detour to the complex plane, where averaging
sets with size equal to the degree exist for all measures and degrees by the Girard-Newton

polynomials up to degree n are integrable. Then there exist z1, ..., zp ∈Cd which form a
complex averaging set of µ of order p and size p. These are unique up to permutation.

To conclude the excursion, we give the following examples, where we calculate the
optimal complex averaging sets from Theorem C.3.
In general, one can derive exact
formulas for up to 4 points, and if the measure is more regular, e.g., real and symmetric,
up to 9 points. Additionally, for measures on the real line, all configurations up to 3
points are also real. This no longer holds for 4 points, as one can see in Proposition C.12.
Examples C.5. Suppose that µ is the measure with unit intensity on the unit disk in
C. Then the optimal complex averaging set from Theorem C.3 just contains 0 multiple
times.

Finally, if we suppose that µ is the Lebesgue measure restricted to [0, 1] like in classical

averaging sets are all real. For 8 points, we showed that the solution also involves complex
numbers which do not lie on the real line. For more points, we can no longer utilize
closed-form solutions for the zeros of symmetric polynomials. However, one can calculate
approximate solutions with high enough accuracy to ensure that they are not purely real,
which we did for many more. What we can observe suggests that there do not exist any
purely real solutions beyond 9 points.

Remark C.9. Theorem C.8 handles the existence of an averaging set of some set B,
degree p, and size n. To use such an averaging set in a construction like in Example 5.7,
we also need to find the averaging set measurably in terms of B. One option is to try
random initializations and use gradient descent to possibly converge to the solution. Since
we showed that the solution exists, and because the squares of polynomials are locally
convex around their zeros, there is a random but finite number of re-initializations after
which a solution can be found in the limit of the gradient descent. Similarly, one can
apply Newton’s method instead of gradient descent. This option is also the one we picked
for the simulations in Subsection 5.1.
In the proof of the preceding Theorem C.8, after some simplifications, one first finds
a quadrature formula by Carath´eodory’s theorem, and then replaces it by an averaging
set with points close to the original nodes, applying [81, Proposition 7.1]. An alternative
way is to start with an approximate averaging set right away, e.g., by using iid points. If
one then uses a base of the polynomials that has orthonormal derivatives, one can make
sure that this approximate averaging set lies in a neighborhood of an exact averaging set.
In this way, one can also find bounds for the minimum number of points of the averaging
set. This technique was applied in [97, 11, 10]. In [10], the authors even derived the
best known bounds with respect to the dimension for averaging sets on the surface of
spheres, i.e., spherical designs. However, if one applies this technique to our setting, the
assumptions on B have to be a lot stricter to guarantee that the points of the resulting
averaging set actually lie in B. On the other hand, one can expect the bound to be a lot
better.
In the following proposition, we show that one can weaken the assumptions in Theo-
rem C.8 by bounding the volume of the convex hull of the set instead of the diameter.
Proposition C.10. For all d ∈N, C > 0, p ∈N0, there exists an N ∈N such that for

D
Hyperfluctuating examples
The goal of this section is to construct novel hyperfluctuating point processes, i.e., point
processes which are not 0-uniform. Our particular interest lies in those examples that
one may expect to be hyperuniform using heuristic arguments, but which are actually
hyperfluctuating instead. We explore these in Appendix D.2. These constructions rely
on the fact that there are hyperfluctuating thinnings of the stationary lattice, which we
construct in Appendix D.1. Further, the point processes we construct are ergodic. This
restriction directly excludes all examples where the only source of hyperfluctuation is an
atom of the Bartlett spectral measure in the origin. These point processes would be trivial
to construct.
D.1
Hyperfluctuating thinnings of the stationary lattice
We first show the existence of hyperfluctuating thinnings of the stationary lattice for d = 1
and then extend the construction to dimensions d ≥2. For d = 1, the construction relies
on renewal processes.

D.2
Hyperfluctuating point processes from the lattice partition
Now we are ready to construct our first example of an ergodic point process which has
exactly one point in each cell of a stationary lattice tiling, but is hyperfluctuating for
d ≥3. Besides the ergodicity, such an example was also given in [21].

of Ψ is invariant under isometries of the lattice.
